No one is perfect. This is why everyone should be canceled. Anyway, American Presidents are not immune to this. This video is about the greatest mistake of every American president while they were in office. They’re worst failure, their biggest oopsie. You get it. When I say “greatest mistake,” I mean the one thing they did that hurt the most people. And when I say “thing they did that hurt the most people,” I say it with my historian hat on, looking at all of American history. So I guess recent Presidents have the advantage of us not truly know the full extent of the damage they’ve done. (sigh) Ok, let’s do this. George Washington Even my favorite President wasn’t perfect. The biggest flaw in his Presidency was his failure to use his influence to call for an end to slavery. By the time he was President, he had long privately said that slavery was bad and needed to go away, but his worry of his own financial security prevented him from freeing his own slaves. Imagine if such a unifying figure like Washington went public about his changed views on slavery and actually freed his slaves during his Presidency. The country would have been so much better off in the following decades. John Adams Adams signed the Alien and Sedition Acts, four of the worst laws in American history. The Naturalization Act made it harder to become a citizen. The Alien Friends Act allowed the president to easily imprison or deport any foreigner who they thought was dangerous. The Alien Enemies Act gave the president even more powers to detain foreigners during a war, and the Sedition Act, which was the worst of them all in my opinion, made it illegal to talk trash or make up fake news about the federal government. I hope you realize that the Sedition Act spit all over the First Amendment. Thomas Jefferson First of all, Jefferson of course was even worse than George Washington when it came to slavery. Sure, for most of his life he called for it to eventually end, and he did sign a law to ban the importation of slaves while President, but he likely fathered multiple enslaved children with one of his young slaves and owned more slaves than any other President in American history. And he didn’t free almost all of them. Ugh, Jefferson. But that all aside, I think Jefferson’s biggest mistake WHILE HE WAS PRESIDENT was signing the Embargo Act of 1807. It banned pretty much all trade with Britain and France. Jefferson supported the law in order to avoid war with both countries, but it ended up devastating the American economy. The country lost tens of millions of dollars and tens of thousands of jobs. James Madison In my video about every president’s greatest accomplishment, I said Madison’s biggest achievement was holding Britain back during the War of 1812. Ironically, though, his biggest mistake was failing to prevent that war from happening in the first place! They called it “Mr. Madison’s War” for good reason. After all, he convinced a divided Congress to move forward with declaring war on a MUCH more powerful country that could easily defeat them if it really wanted to. It was reckless, to say the least. James Monroe Monroe’s biggest mistake was signing the Missouri Compromise. I used to be more indifferent about the law, but now I say it was actually a major cause of the Civil War. The Missouri Compromise admitted Missouri as a slave state and Maine as a free state to maintain the equal balance of slave states and free states that had already existed. It also prohibited slavery north of a line of latitude west of the southern border of Missouri. Despite the fact that Monroe had expressed SEVERAL times that he wanted slavery eventually abolished, this law, in fact, made the institution more permanent. Sure, it avoided a national crisis, but it only kicked the can down the road. The crisis would ONLY get worse later on and Monroe could have prevented it! John Quincy Adams You all know me. I’m still the same ol’ Teach. I hate tariffs because they make stuff more expensive for ordinary folks like you and me. As it turns out, JQ’s biggest mistake was signing the Tariff of 1828, or as I and other critics like to call it, The Tariff of Abominations. It hurt both industry and farming alike, and even led to the Nullification Crisis. Andrew Jackson Of course his signing of the Indian Removal Act and causing the Trail of Tears was horrible, but I think Jackson’s ill-informed hard money policies ultimately hurt even more people. He refused to establish a new Bank of the United States, instead allowing state banks to just freely loan paper money to pretty much anyone who asked for it. In response to THIS crisis, Jackson issued an executive order that required all payment for federal government land be paid for in either gold or silver. Well this caused inflation to SOAR. Eventually this all at least partially led to the Panic of 1837 and one of the worst depressions in American history. Normally the President has little control over the overall economy, but Jackson’s actions certainly played a big role causing this economic crisis. Martin Van Buren So what was the Trail of Tears? Well, it was a series of forced displacements of around 60,000 people from the Cherokee, Muscogee, Seminole, Chickasaw, and Choctaw nations. They were forced out of their homes in in the Southeast and marched out west for thousands of miles. Most ended up in future Oklahoma. The relocated suffered from disease and starvation on the way traveling often through harsh conditions. Andrew Jackson may have signed the Indian Removal Act and started the Trail of Tears, but Martin Van Buren certainly carried out the most infamous part of it, the removal of the Cherokee from Georgia. At least 4,000 of THEM died, many of them babies or small children, as they traveled on foot through a particularly harsh winter, most with no shoes on and little clothing. William Henry Harrison Yeah Harrison’s biggest mistake while President was drinking the DC water. That’s apparently what got him sick and led to his death just a month into his presidency. John Tyler Believe it or not, Tyler didn’t make many mistakes as President. Then again, he didn’t accomplish much as President, either. I think his biggest mistake was refusing to re-establish a national bank. He even vetoed a bill to charter one. While central banks have not made things completely all rosy throughout history, most economists argue that they have kept markets stable and helped prevent economic crashes. In fact, that aforementioned Panic of 1837 likely would have been avoided if there had been a central bank in place to regulate banking transactions and help failing banks. After Tyler vetoed the creation of a new central bank, the country would experience some of its worst recessions and bank runs for the rest of the century, man. James Polk Polk’s biggest mistake was starting an unjust war with Mexico. Polk was basically an imperialist who knew that if he sent troops into disputed territory along the Texas-Mexico border, he would likely be starting a war. In fact, I’d argue he hoped it would happen. To Mexico, it was an invasion. It was completely reasonable for Mexico to attack American troops, as matter of fact, which they did on April 25, 1846. Polk couldn’t have designed it any better. He went to Congress urging them to declare war on Mexico, claiming that they “shed American blood upon American soil.” Congress, of course, went along with it. But it’s long past due that we all admit that the Mexican-American war was a war of American aggression just seeking to expand their borders. Zachary Taylor Taylor was only in office for 16 months, but that was enough to make mistakes. Taylor’s biggest mistake was raising tensions in the months leading up to the Compromise of 1850. Despite being a slave owner himself, Taylor rejected any compromise with the slave states to let them expand slavery out west as originally agreed under the Missouri Compromise. I appreciated Taylor taking anti-slavery positions, but his rhetoric against southern leaders only sped up the country’s path toward civil war, which I guess...yeah it was inevitable by this point anyway. Millard Fillmore After Taylor died in office, Fillmore took over and SUPPORTED the Compromise of 1850, except that he was perfectly fine with the WORST PART OF IT. He signed the Fugitive Slave Act, which made it so that the federal government was responsible for finding and returning escaped slaves, even if they were in a free state. Yep, free states had to cooperate. This was definitely a law that directly led to the Civil War. Uh-huh. Franklin Pierce Speaking of laws that led directly to the Civil War. The Kansas-Nebraska Act! Pierce signed that law, which is easily one of the worst federal laws ever passed in American history. It opened Kansas for settlement and allowed the new settlers to determine whether it would be admitted as a slave state or a free state. Naturally, this caused a mad rush of proslavery and antislavery advocates to Kansas, and violence would often occur between the two groups. That’s how the territory earned the nickname “Bleeding Kansas.” Pierce literally caused the prequel to the Civil War. James Buchanan Sure, Buchanan was a corrupt dude who did some shady stuff to help only him and his friends while in office, but his BIGGEST mistake was allowing Southern states to secede. Not only did he refuse to send down federal troops to prevent them from doing so, he didn’t even talk trash about Southern leaders when they began to do so. He was like “oh well, I personally don’t think seceding is a good idea but you do you, South.” So Buchanan didn’t just do NOTHING to prevent the Civil War, he also did SOMETHING to cause it! Isn’t that something? Abraham Lincoln You might already be noticing a pattern here with me. You mess with the First Amendment, I got problems with you, and Lincoln certainly abused the First Amendment. He arrested journalists and newspaper publishers who criticized his handling of the Civil War. His administration censored mail and telegraphs that undermined the war. Lincoln had people arrested singing Confederate songs, for crying out loud. I don’t care if it was war time. That’s messed up, man. Andrew Johnson Uh yeah, Johnson made PLENTY of mistakes, but I think his biggest one was causing the Black Codes to happen. The Black Codes were laws passed by Southern states designed to make the lives of recently freed slaves miserable. Essentially, slavery was now illegal after the passing of the 13th Amendment, but the Black Codes made it so African Americans still FELT enslaved. After the Civil War ended, Johnson allowed proslavery Southern legislatures to quickly take back over and yeah, nearly all of them passed black codes. Ulysses Grant Grant’s biggest mistake was supporting and enforcing the Comstock laws. It all started with his signing of the Comstock Act of 1873, which made it illegal to mail stuff or receive stuff in the mail that was “obscene,” “ lewd”, “immoral,” or “indecent.” It was another slap in the face of the First Amendment. After Grant signed the law, a dude named Anthony Comstock, who all these laws were named after, became THE ENFORCER against bad stuff in the mail. He arrested an activist named Ezra Heywood after Heywood mailed one of his pamphlets which made the claim that women should have the right to control their own bodies. Comstock also arrested another dude for RECEIVING that pamphlet in the mail. Rutherford Hayes Ok, so Hayes, I get WHY you did this as part of the Compromise of 1877, but it still sucked that you did it. Hayes’ biggest mistake was removing federal troops from the South, marking the end of Reconstruction and directly leading to a dramatic rise of white supremacist rule throughout the South. African Americans no longer had federal protection, and for decades would be treated in the South as second class citizens. James Garfield Garfield’s biggest mistake was letting the media know his schedule. That’s how Charles Guiteau (gee toe) knew Garfield was going to be at the Baltimore and Potomac Railroad station on July 2, 1881. There, Guiteau shot Garfield. Now, Garfield’s OTHER biggest mistake was trusting his doctor, Willard Bliss. Garfield was actually starting to recover before Bliss screwed things up. While trying to get his bullet out with unsterilized fingers and tools, he caused a severe infection, which ultimately led to Garfield dying from septic shock. Chester Arthur Arthur’s biggest mistake was signing the Chinese Exclusion Act, another one of the worst laws in American history. It banned the immigration of Chinese who were coming over to do manual, unskilled labor, which uh…the country desperately needed help with by the way. It led to similar laws that further discriminated against Chinese Americans in the coming decades. Not only that, it led to what’s known as the “Driving Out” era, a period in which anti-Chinese Americans assaulted and even killed Chinese Americans if they didn’t get out of their communities. The bottom line was, Arthur signed the Chinese Exclusion Act because of xenophobia…an irrational fear of Chinese people. Grover Cleveland How about another crappy law? Cleveland’s biggest mistake was signing the Dawes Act. The law said the President could subdivide Native American tribal communal landholdings into portions for individual heads of families to privately own and opened up leftover plots to the general public to settle. This law singlehandedly led to the tribes across the country losing around 62% of their land. But its main goal was to force them to be more like Americans. Benjamin Harrison Harrison’s biggest mistake also hurt Native Americans, and I’m not talking about his proclamation opening up Indian Territory to American settlement. I’m talking about the fact that, under his authority, the U.S. Army murdered around 146 Sioux on December 29, 1890 on the Pine Ridge reservation in South Dakota. Today we call this the Wounded Knee Massacre, and not only did Harrison approve of it…but he gave out Medals of Honor to 20 of the American soldiers guilty of the massacre. Geez dude. William McKinley McKinley’s biggest mistake was the Philippine-American War, a war so messed up that you wouldn’t believe what happened in it if I told twenty times. It was an unjust war from the very start. The Filipinos had just gained independence from Spain and McKinely now wanted the United States to control them. Of course they resisted, and between 1899 and 1902 perhaps as many as 1 million Filipino civilians died because of it. American forces burned villages, threw Filipinos into concentration camps, and tortured Flilipinos who were suspected of rebelling against Americans. There’s other horrible stuff American troops did, but I won’t go into detail in case there are any kids watching. This war is one of the ugliest parts of American history, and McKinley was responsible for it. Theodore Roosevelt As much as I talk trash about Teddy’s crappy foreign policy, I give him mad props for it improving throughout his Presidency. He was a man who changed his mind when learning new information, after all. However, one thing still stands out as a pretty big mistake during his Presidency- The Brownsville affair. In Brownsville, Texas in August 1906, after someone killed a white bartender and injured a white police officer, the town’s residents all decided to blame all the members of the African-American 25th Infantry Regiment even though there was no evidence they actually did it. Well, Roosevelt thought they were guilty, ordering 167 of the 25th Infantry Regiment’s soldiers dishonorably discharged. In other words, Teddy didn’t allow them to have due process of the law. They were guilty until proven innocent. By the way, the Army found them innocent in 1972. By then only two of them were still alive. William Howard Taft Taft’s biggest mistake was signing the Payne-Aldrich Tariff Act, a law that mostly raised tariffs and was so controversial that it deeply split the Republican Party. Taft himself was quite proud of the new law, later calling it “the best tariff bill the Republican Party ever passed.” While it did lower tariffs on some items, it raised it on others and ultimately just frustrated everyone. It also made the Philippines heavily dependent on the United States. Woodrow Wilson Wilson’s biggest mistake (Wilson!) Yes. There were MANY to choose from for Wilson, but I think his biggest mistake was signing of the Espionage and Sedition Acts. Passed after the United States entered World War One, the Espionage Act made it illegal to interfere in the war effort in any way. The Sedition Act made it a crime to say “disloyal, profane, scurrilous or abusive” stuff about the American government, armed forces, or even its flag. According to the historian Paul Avrich, as many as 1000 people went to prison as a result of the Espionage And Sedition Acts signed by Wilson. Some of them got sentences up to 20 years in prison. Again, these are laws that, in my view, strongly went against the First Amendment. Warren Harding The 1920s were NOT good in terms of xenophobia and anti-immigrant legislation in general, and Harding definitely played a role in that. His biggest mistake was signing the Emergency Quota Act, a law restricting immigration from certain countries that were “undesirable.” In reality, it was just meant to restrict immigrants coming from Eastern and Southern Europe. That said, I also have to give two dishonorable mentions for Harding. He signed the crappy Fordney-McCumber Tariff…hey you know how I feel about tariffs…and the Revenue Act of 1921, which was really just a law super wealthy people wanted passed since they didn’t like paying taxes. Calvin Coolidge Speaking of crappy immigration laws, Coolidge’s biggest mistake was signing the Immigration Act of 1924, which expanded xenophobia to Asia. It HEAVILY restricted immigration from Asia. It’s even also called the Asian Exclusion Act, by the way. Together with the Emergency Quota Act, the Immigration Act of 1924 favored immigrants from Northern and Western Europe…for some reason…I guess because their skin color tended to be lighter? Anyway, the xenophobic immigration system solidified by this law stayed in place until the Immigration and Naturalization Act of 1965. Immigrants have historically made the United States a much stronger place, and Coolidge’s Immigration Act essentially shut the door on most immigrants. Herbert Hoover The Great Depression! No, we can’t blame the entire Great Depression on poor Hoover. That would be silly. Uh, but he did make it worse. Hoover’s biggest mistake was the Smoot-Hawley Tariff Act, which was supposed to encourage people to buy American-made stuff by placing tariffs and thus increasing the cost of imported stuff.. Instead, it led to trading partners retaliating by raising THEIR tariff rates and ultimately froze international trade, causing companies’ sales to drop everywhere. In fact, this one law dramatically made the Great Depression worse. Soon after Hoover signed it, the banks began to fail. By the end of 1931, nearly 2,300 banks had failed, and $1.7 billion in deposits were lost. Franklin Roosevelt FDR’s biggest mistake was forcing more than 125,000 Japanese Americans to live in concentration camps, completely stripping them of their freedoms, despite no evidence that any of them were conspiring to help Japan in World War Two. If you want to learn more about it, I have a video here. Oh and another one here. Then again, you’re probably already depressed enough by this point and have already lost faith in humanity. So maybe you don’t wanna learn more about this stuff. Harry Truman Truman’s biggest mistake was The Cold War. Wait, the ENTIRE Cold War?!? Well, kind of yeah. He escalated tensions that contributed to the Cold War happening in the first place. For starters, his foreign policy, famously known as the Truman Doctrine, called for military interventions all over the world in the name of “support for democracies against authoritarian threats.” Say, did that mean at home, too? Anyway, whether it was getting the United States involved in the Korean War, giving military and economic aid to countries to fight governments friendly to the Soviet Union, quickly expanding the development of nuclear weapons, or destroying any chance of diplomacy with Soviet leaders, Truman’s actions all played a big role increasing tensions between the two countries. Don’t get me wrong, I’d blame the Soviet Union’s government just as much for creating the Cold War. They were far from innocent…but Truman’s actions also played a key role. I always cut Truman some slack, though. He was president during a TREMENDOUSLY difficult time in history. Dwight Eisenhower Look, as you may already know, (“I Like Ike”), but his presidency wasn’t without mistakes. Particularly his reckless roundup of undocumented Mexican immigrants along the southern border and him signing Executive Order 10450, which contributed to the Lavender Scare. However, I think Ike’s biggest mistake was allowing the CIA to help overthrow the democratically elected Prime Minister Mohammad Mosaddegh in favor of the Shah, Mohammad Reza Pahlavi, aka Operation Ajax. It was the first time the CIA directly orchestrated a coup of a democratically elected government, and it later led to a more radical government getting in there after the Iranian Revolution, which itself then led to the Iran Hostage Crisis. The bottom line is, Iranians would NEVER forget the American government’s involvement in overthrowing their popular leader. Even today, the governments of the United States and Iran have a poor relationship, and it all goes back to Operation Ajax. John F. Kennedy JFK’s biggest mistake was the Bay of Pigs Invasion, which was a failed military operation in which Cuban exiles landed in Cuba in order to attempt to overthrow Cuba’s communist government, led by Fidel Castro. Most historians acknowledge the invasion as one of the biggest foreign policy failures in American history. It made Castro even more of a hero and increased Cold War tensions with the Soviet Union. Not only that, it made Cuba a closer ally of the Soviet Union, setting the stage for the Cuban Missile Crisis the very next year. Lyndon Johnson LBJ’s biggest mistake was the escalation of troops in the Vietnam War. Not only do most historians view the war as a costly disaster, LBJ’s justification for dramatically increasing troop levels there to fight it was partially based on a straight up lie. Congress agreed to escalate troops there after the two attacks that together became known as the Gulf of Tonkin incident. As it turns out, American soldiers provoked the first attack, and the second attack….well that never happened. LBJ and his administration completely lied about it. So ultimately, tens of thousands of American troops ended up dying based on this lie. Richard Nixon You might be thinking that Watergate was Nixon’s biggest mistake, or perhaps his secret bombing of Cambodia and Laos as revealed in the Pentagon Papers, but I’d argue him starting the War on Drugs was his biggest mistake. It was more than just words. Nixon created the Office of Drug Abuse Law Enforcement, which eventually morphed into the Drug Enforcement Administration, or DEA, who mostly went after nonviolent drug offenders. Incarceration rates have skyrocketed ever since, with people of color disproportionately being arrested and convicted with harsher sentences than other groups even though they used drugs at the same rate. In fact, many argue Nixon’s War on Drugs was mostly racially motivated. Nixon signed the Controlled Substances Act, which criminalized drug abuse and said, without any evidence whatsoever, that marijuana and psilocybin were just as dangerous as heroin and more dangerous than cocaine. Ever since, people who have needed medical treatment have instead been sent to prison with VIOLENT offenders. According to some estimates, the United States has spent more than ONE TRILLION DOLLARS fighting the war on drugs. And the drugs have won the war, by the way. Drug use is arguably as common as ever. Gerald Ford Ford wasn’t president long enough to do much damage, but I’d argue his biggest mistake was his support of the brutal Indonesian dictator Suharto and his invasion of East Timor from 1975 to 1976. It led to tens of thousands of civilians dying, and Ford gave Suharto the weapons that led to those deaths. Jimmy Carter This one was easy, and Carter later even admitted this as a big mistake during his presidency- how he handled the Iran Hostage Crisis, which I have a video about, of course. I’m always making videos and stuff. No matter what Carter seemed to do, whether it be economic pressure, diplomacy, or even sending in the military to try to rescue them, things just didn’t go right to rescue those 63 American hostages. Ronald Reagan Simply put, Reagan’s biggest mistake might just be something named after him- Reaganomics. Reaganomics is basically the economic policies popularized and associated with Reagan, but they’re nothing new. Ya know, stuff like deregulation, tightening the money supply to fight inflation, and reducing the federal income tax and capital gains taxes. And to be honest, I’m personally a fan of all that stuff, at least to a certain extent. However, Reaganomics took all that to the extreme, and many economists say that his policies dramatically reduced economic mobility, meaning ever since it’s been much more difficult for Americans to get out of poverty. Economists also argue Reaganomics has led to a widening income gap…the rich getting richer and poor getting poorer basically. It also has definitely led to a general trend of the national debt spiraling out of control. George H.W. Bush Although a dishonorable mention ought to go to Operation Just Cause, which was when the United States invaded Panama to overthrow their leader Manuel Noriega, I think Bush Sr.’s biggest mistake was pardoning six of the convicted criminals associated with the Iran–Contra affair. Simply put, I think it greatly undermined the idea that no person is above the law. There’s a vid about the Iran Contra Affair. I’m sorry. All this homework for ya. Bill Clinton Clinton usually gets remembered for his personal scandals, especially the Monika Lewinsky scandal that ultimately led to him being impeached, but I think some of the LAWS he signed were much more damaging for the country. Three laws stand out to me, in particular, as doing way more harm than good: The Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act, aka the 1994 Crime Bill, the Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Act, and the Telecommunications Act of 1996. While the Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Act had good aspects, like decreasing unemployment, saving the federal government money, and decentralizing welfare management, it completely ignored existing systemic problems, and has arguably led to even more poverty among people of color and more nonviolent drug offenders having a more difficult time after they get out of prison. The Telecommunications Act of 1996 caused a small number of giant corporations to control nearly all media, making it much more difficult these days for independent musicians, filmmakers, and artists to have their stuff discovered. But Clinton's biggest mistake was signing the 1994 Crime Bill, which gave money to states to build new prisons and hire new police officers, but also led to MUCH stricter prison sentences overall. Because of that law, tens of thousands of people got far more harsh punishments than they deserved. And just like the Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Act, the 1994 Crime Bill made systemic racism much worse. People of color got arrested more than whites and got harsher sentences than whites for the EXACT SAME CRIMES. By the way, Clinton himself has admitted that signing this law was his biggest mistake. George W. Bush This is another easy one. Bush Jr.’s biggest mistake was Operation Iraqi Freedom. Bush’s invasion of Iraq in 2003 was mostly a huge failure based on lies. The Iraq War and War in Iraq both devastated the country, leading to the deaths of hundreds of thousands of innocent people and tens of billions of dollars of damage, and Bush and his administration were mostly to blame for it. Barack Obama Obama’s biggest mistake was the expansion and normalization of the use of armed drones. He dramatically increased drone warfare, targeting suspected terrorists in multiple countries, often ignoring the sovereignty of those countries. And he dropped many of these bombs on innocent civilians. In fact, as much as more than 15% of the casualties were civilians. He normalized the use of drone warfare, which I think…frankly…is pretty messed up. Related to this, Obama killed this 16-year old American citizen with a drone strike. Now, it may have been an accident, but geez man. The kid didn’t do anything wrong. This was murder. Donald Trump Oh Trump loved drone warfare, too. He possibly killed even more civilians than Obama did, but we won’t know for a really long time since he kept all that information secret. It’s classified, baby. But what’s not secret is his blatant attempts to overturn the legitimate presidential election of 2020. After he lost the election, he repeatedly lied about there being widespread voter fraud, and in the process riled up his devoted followers, creating political chaos across the country that culminated with the January 6 United States Capitol attack. By undermining the 2020 election, he put our entire electoral system in jeopardy. Even today, tens of millions of Americans believe there was widespread voter fraud, and it’s only mostly because Trump insisted there was. It’s not because of their so-called “evidence”, because I’ve looked at the so-called “evidence" and it’s crap…I’m sorry, there’s nothing there of substance. Joe Biden While the withdrawal from Afghanistan was a disaster, that would have probably been a disaster no matter who was in charge. Therefore, I’m going with his forcing private companies with 100 or more employees to enforce a vaccine mandate. It eventually got struck down by the Supreme Court as it should have been in my opinion. But hey, Biden’s currently still in office. I’m sure if he tries hard enough, he can make an even bigger mistake! And there you have it. Every President’s biggest mistake. I should stress that these are their biggest mistakes in my opinion. If you feel that I’VE made a mistake about these mistakes, do not be mistaken, you can comment your mistakes below. (awkward pause) Hopefully it was not a mistake to make this video. If you enjoyed this video, you’re weird, but remember, that’s a compliment. Anyway, you’ll probably also enjoy the video in which I go over every American President’s Greatest Accomplishment. (looking up at the thumbnail) I’m so much more positive in that video. Now it’s time for my monthly shout out to all my Patreon supporters who donate at least $15 or more each month to my channel. Starting with my biggest donors, thank you to Bill Dowd, Pat Iapicca, Adam Luckey, Alicia Solberg, Andrew B., Anthony Beckett, Austin Siros, Corey Rieman, Douglas Martin, Dr. Paul J. Lilly, emptymachine, Isabelle, Isaiah Warfield, Joe Cook, Matt Standish, Nik Everett, Osbers Gaming, Owen, Sami, Samuel Striz, Sean Conant, Society’s Basement, Adam Christians, Andrew Schneider, Grant Hughes, Ian Driscoll, KkkaSenjika615, Ilan Capone, Jack L, Jakob Birnbaum, Joel Serrano Lozada, KZ210QB, Oliver E., LT Marshall, Naterade, Stacey, Steve Bryan, Thomas Oppenheim, Warren Jarog, Waterfort, and Zachary F. Parker. Thank you to ALL my Patreon supporters, and thank YOU for watching.

**Structure:** List/rundown format, chronological by president
**Framing:** Mock-anger comedic cold open (character break) followed by self-aware meta commentary before the historical content

### 6. Every President's Education (Oct 22, 2021) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hDjCm6ll1gI)
**Metrics:** 3.4M views (rounded, list view), 42,174 likes (42:55)
**Opening hook (verbatim, transcript):**
> Who's educated? I'm Mr. Beat I am what you call an "educated guy." Whatever that means, right. Is it because I unironically use words like erroneous in casual conversation? Is it because I have three "degrees?" Anyway, yeah I'm probably not that smart. But what about all the American Presidents?

**Full transcript (verbatim):**
> Who’s educated? I’m Mr. Beat I am what you call an “educated guy.” Whatever that means, right. Is it because I unironically use words like erroneous in casual conversation? Is it because I have three “degrees?” Is it because I have a “Master’s” degree? Maybe I don't "look the part." I'm not "svelt." I don't "look comfortable on camera." Anyway, yeah I’m probably not that smart. But what about all the American Presidents? Well, it’s safe to say, we’ve had a lot of really smart Presidents throughout American history. We know what intelligence is. Kind of. Ok here’s the definition: “the ability to acquire and apply knowledge and skills.” Oh I like that. But here’s the thing. Intelligence is a really tricky thing to measure. I mean, we’ve tried to measure intelligence. We have lots of different kinds of intelligence tests, but pretty much all of them have flaws. There’s also something called an intelligence quotient, more commonly referred to by its initials, IQ. But since IQs are assigned to people based on flawed intelligence tests, even IQs should be looked at suspiciously. In fact, some scientists have argued that an IQ test does NOT measure a person’s intelligence that well at all, as it turns out. In other words, a high IQ doesn’t necessarily mean you’re smart. So what about schooling then? Is a person who went to school longer or received a more rigorous training more intelligent than another person who didn’t? I know this is anecdotal, but some of the smartest people I’ve ever met dropped out of high school. Maybe schooling is overrated. Regardless, we have nothing else to go off of for the Presidents, so let’s just roll with it, ok? And please know that we are NOT going to even attempt to say who the smartest President was in this video because that would be impossible. I’ll let you speculate, though, for the fun of it. Instead, in this video, we are just looking at “how educated” each President was. Well, let’s throw in IQ in there, too, just for the fun of it. But yeah, for each President in American history, let’s look at where they went to school, or...uh...something that resembled school, and their estimated IQ. In case you’re wondering, I got the IQ information from a dude named Dean Keith Simonton who researched this stuff at the University of California at Davis and published his estimates back in 2006. In his paper, Dr. Simonton went through his methodology and explained exactly how he reached his conclusions, but basically he just used a statistical algorithm after examining biographies, This dude, The Dude, I should say, never graduated from college, believe it or not. In fact, he never graduated from high school. In fact, he never graduated from middle school. He basically received the modern equivalent of a grade school education. Now, later, he did receive a surveyor’s certificate from the College of William and Mary, I should say. This lack of formal education, though, kind of hurt Washington throughout his early career. This is probably why later in life he strongly called for expanding formal education to give more folks opportunities, and his will left money to three different schools. His estimated IQ was 132.5, putting him in the middle of the pack. John Adams Harvard, baby. Yep, after attending top notch schools as a kid, he entered Harvard College at 16 years old and graduated at 20. Adams was one of five Presidents to graduate from Harvard. By the time he was 23, he had a master’s degree from Harvard and used it to become a very successful lawyer. Adams was always a hard-working and excellent student. Good job, John. His estimated IQ was 142.5, which was one of the highest IQs of all Presidents. Thomas Jefferson Jefferson also received a really good formal education. He began studying at The College of William and Mary when he was 17, and spent the next seven years training to become a lawyer. They called it a “gentleman’s education,” because he never technically got a degree there, but he did get an appropriate education. However, later in life, he got four different honorary degrees. He was one of the earliest and most passionate and vocal supporters of creating public school systems. Really though, Jefferson was truly a life-long student. He never stopped learning, and remained tremendously curious until the day he died. Jefferson already has a reputation for being one of the smartest Presidents, and his estimated IQ was 153.75, which is the second highest of all Presidents. James Madison Another smart dude. And Princeton, baby. After also attending top notch schools as a kid, he began attending the College of New Jersey when he was 18. Yeah today that college is called Princeton. There he studied Latin, Greek, theology, and Philosophy, with an emphasis on the works of the Enlightenment. His studies at Princeton greatly influenced his political philosophy, which you may already know is a big reason why we have THE FREAKING CONSTITUTION. Similar to his good friend Jefferson, Madison was also a life-long student who never stopped learning. His estimated IQ was 141.25, which was one of the highest IQs of all Presidents. James Monroe Education wasn’t quite as important to Mr. Monroe, who dropped out of the College of William and Mary. Hey, but he did have a good reason to drop out. He dropped out to fight in the American War of Independence. And after the war, he continued to study law, and while doing so was fortunate to have the aforementioned Thomas Jefferson as his mentor. Connections, connections, connections. His estimated IQ was 124.1, which was one of the lowest IQs of all Presidents. John Quincy Adams I do have to mention that many historians do say JQ was our smartest President. With his dad being the aforementioned President John Adams, of course JQ also went to nice schools growing up. During the American Revolution, he went to Europe with his dad and continued studying, spending a brief time at Passy Academy in France and later the University of Leiden in the Netherlands. However, he wasn’t completely happy in Europe, and went back to his home, the new country of the United States to transfer to Harvard, where his dad went. Also like his dad, he studied law. Adams had a habit of reading as much as humanly possible. To do this, he got good at speed reading and took notes by getting good at shorthand, basically using a bunch of abbreviations and symbols to write quicker. And yeah, his estimated IQ was 168.75, the highest IQ of all Presidents. Andrew Jackson The area where Jackson grew up in the Carolinas had little opportunities for formal schooling. Not that his family could afford to send him to some fancy school anyway. No one knows exactly how much schooling he got. We do know he never was that good at writing or spelling, and he was an orphan by the time he was 14, even losing his two brothers during the American War of Independence. That all said, he did study law at an office in Salisbury, North Carolina, later becoming quite a successful lawyer. His estimated IQ was 126.25, which was one of the lowest IQs of all Presidents. Martin Van Buren Van Buren also had fewer opportunities growing up, as his family never made much money. He did attend a one-room schoolhouse in his home town of Kinderhook, New York, until he was 14. He never went to college, but his dad did pull some strings to get him connected with a lawyer’s office as a law clerk. For seven years, he did grunt work there by day but studied law by night. After moving to New York City, Van Buren passed the state bar exam to become a lawyer. His estimated IQ was 133.4, putting him in the middle of the pack. William Henry Harrison Harrison had a pretty good education and even got a tutor at home growing up. He studied for three years at Hampden-Sydney College in Virginia, before his dad pressured him into studying to become a doctor in Philadelphia under the mentorship of the legendary doctor and Founding Father Benjamin Rush. However, after his father died shortly after he moved to Philadelphia, Harrison was like “I don’t really want to become a doctor anyway,” so he left medical school to pursue the military career he really always wanted. His estimated IQ was 133.9, which places him in the upper half of the pack. John Tyler Tyler was another Virginian who, like Jefferson and Monroe before him, went to the College of William and Mary, graduating at the young age of 17. He studied law and passed the state bar exam to become a lawyer at just 19. Technically he was too young to become a lawyer in Virginia at the time, by the way, but the judge who approved of it never asked Tyler how old he was. Tyler’s estimated IQ was 136.2, which also places him in the upper half of the pack. James Polk Polk didn’t get much formal schooling since he was sickly growing up. At 18, he barely knew how to read or write, but he really kicked it into gear at that age, studying his butt off and becoming proficient in English, Greek, and Latin. He ended up graduating from the University of North Carolina with first honors in both mathematics and classics. Afterward, he studied law in Nashville, Tennessee, under a big time lawyer named Felix Grundy, who later was a U.S. Senator. Connections, connections, connections. Polk’s estimated IQ was 130.2, which places him in the lower half of the pack. Zachary Taylor Meh, Taylor never was that much excited about school. Although he went to top notch schools growing up, he was not a good student. For most of his life, his handwriting, spelling, and grammar were particularly bad. Similar to William Henry Harrison, he wanted to be a military man from a very young age, and he skipped college altogether to pursue that. Although a very skilled general, his estimated IQ was 125.7, which was one of the lowest IQs of all Presidents. Millard Fillmore Fillmore grew up in poverty, and due to that had very little formal schooling growing up. He mostly had to work on the family farm. However, he still had lots of ambition and more importantly to this video...lots of CURIOSITY. Even though his dad tried to get him into the cloth trade as a teenager, Millard hated it, instead using what little money he had to buy a dictionary and stealing books to teach himself how to read. He eventually met a teacher named Abigail Powers, who loaned him books and helped him study. They also fell in love, and eventually got married, by the way. While Millard never went to college, he read lots of books and Abigail also helped him study to become a lawyer. He passed the bar exam and became a lawyer when he was 23 years old. Fillmore’s estimated IQ was 136, which places him in the upper half of the pack. Franklin Pierce Pierce attended pretty decent schools growing up, especially as a teenager. When he was just 15, he began going to Bowdoin College in Brunswick, Maine. That’s where he met his friend, the famous writer Nathaniel Hawthorne, by the way. At first, he apparently partied too much at Bowdoin, but eventually he began to apply himself, becoming one of the top students in his class. After graduating from Bowdoin, he briefly went to Northampton Law School before dropping out, but still becoming a lawyer on his own a couple years later anyway after studying with influential family friends. Connections, connections, connections. His estimated IQ was 134.8, which also places him in the upper half of the pack. James Buchanan Buchanan went to solid schools growing up. At 16 years old, he began attending Dickinson College in Carlisle, Pennsylvania. Even though he nearly got expelled from the school a couple times due to misbehavior, he managed to graduate with honors two years later, and immediately began studying law, although independently. By the time he was 22 he had passed the bar exam and become a lawyer. His estimated IQ was 125.9, which was one of the lowest IQs of all Presidents. Abraham Lincoln I think it’s also well known that Lincoln, often considered by historians as the greatest President in American history, had a uniquely American educational background. Lincoln had very little formal schooling. Maybe a year of it, at most. Lincoln later called his own education “defective.” But similar to the aforementioned Jefferson, Madison, and Fillmore, he was a very curious man. So while he was self educated, his love of learning, more than anything, took him far. While he grew up working on farms, splitting wood, and just doing plain old hard labor, throughout that time he had taught himself to read and write, often with the encouragement of his stepmother, Sally, who shared many books with him. Indeed, Lincoln read whatever the heck he could get his hands on. That eventually led to him becoming a lawyer, passing the bar exam at the age of 25. His estimated IQ was 140, which places him in the upper half of the pack. Not bad for a man who never went to school. Andrew Johnson Johnson also had no formal schooling growing up. As I sang in my song about him (“his future wife taught him how to write and read, but this was not until he was 17”) Yep, that’d be Eliza McCardle. By trade, he ran a tailor shop, but his interest in politics is what motivated him to learn more about the world. Oh, and his desire to stick it to the rich and powerful. His estimated IQ was 125.7, which was one of the lowest IQs of all Presidents. Ulysses Grant Yeah Grant wasn’t a big fan of school. He also wasn’t a fan of his father’s tannery, where he worked as a child. He did, however, love horses and horsemanship. He also loved traveling. These interests, combined with the fact that his family couldn’t afford to send him to college, led him to the military. Fortunately for him, he was accepted into the prestigious United States Military Academy at West Point. Overall, he was a mediocre student, but he was decent at math and drawing, and everyone was impressed with his superior horse riding abilities, which later helped him get a spot in the Army’s cavalry. Despite being one of the most skilled generals on the battlefield in American history, Grant had the lowest estimated IQ of all Presidents, with a score of 120. Rutherford Hayes Rutherford’s mom, Sophia, taught him how to read and write. His sister, Fanny, was smart, and also helped teach him the classics at home. Rutherford was one of the first Presidents to attend public schools. When he went they were chaotic places with all ages of kids mixed together. Later he went to private schools, which helped him get into Kenyon College to pursue a...you guessed it...legal career. He was an excellent student. In fact, he was valedictorian of the class of 1842. After he graduated from Kenyon, he got into Harvard Law School, just like Elle Woods later would, graduating from there when he was 23 and passing the bar exam to become a lawyer soon after that. His estimated IQ was 133.9, putting him in the middle of the pack. James Garfield There is no doubt that Garfield was one of our smartest Presidents. In fact, he has been referred to as “The Education President,” as a huge advocate for making education more accessible to all citizens, and he was even a teacher himself for a short time. Born into poverty, he often found escape in a rough upbringing through reading books. When he was 16, he ran away from home to attempt to become a sailor, but that didn’t last long since he got sick and he had to return home. When he was 17, he began attending a seminary, which is where he learned he was a pretty good preacher. Later, he studied at the Eclectic Institute in Hiram, Ohio, while simultaneously earning a living as a janitor. At 23, he transferred to Williams College in Massachusetts, graduating with honors at 25 and returning to teach a wide variety of courses at the Eclectic Institute, including English, Latin, Greek, history, geology, and even mathematics. He later studied law on his own and passed the Ohio bar exam when he was 30. He was ambidextrous, and he spoke several languages. Garfield’s estimated IQ was 141.5, which was one of the highest IQs of all Presidents. Chester Arthur Much of Arthur’s schooling took place at home, although he did attend schools as a teenager. Later, he decided to become a...you guessed it...lawyer, passing the bar exam when he was 25 and, thanks to his father, got a clerkship in a big New York City legal firm. Connections, connections, connections. Arthur’s estimated IQ was 141.5, which was the same as Garfield’s, one of the highest IQs of all Presidents. Grover Cleveland Cleveland also never went to college. He did attend private schools growing up, but after his father died when he was 16, he left school to find work to support his family. It was there that he decided to become a...you guessed it...a lawyer ok? He basically took a clerkship at a firm and worked his butt off, eventually studying on his own to pass the bar exam, becoming a lawyer when he was 22. He later taught at Princeton. He then served in the American Civil War, which is where he met future President Rutherford Hayes. After the war ended, McKinley returned to Ohio to become a...you guessed it...lawyer, studying at Albany Law School in New York. He returned to Ohio to pass the bar exam when he was 24. McKinley’s estimated IQ was 130.2, which places him in the lower half of the pack. Theodore Roosevelt A sickly boy, Roosevelt grew up tutored at home by some pretty darn good tutors. There he studied all kinds of stuff, including German, history, zoology, forensics, and composition. He also was an athlete there as a wrestler and boxer. He actually met his first wife, Alice Hathaway Lee, in college. After graduating from Harvard, he attended Columbia Law School, but dropped out after one year to get into politics. He later got an honorary degree from Columbia. Like much later. In 2008. So he was dead by then. Northwestern gave him an honorary degree as well. His estimated IQ was 142.3, which was one of the highest IQs of all Presidents. William Howard Taft Growing up, his parents pressured him a lot when it came to education. Mostly due to his continued hard work and dedication to his studies, he graduated second in his class when he was 20, going on to Cincinnati Law School. When he was 16, he started attending Davidson College near Charlotte, North Carolina, getting good at logic, public speaking, Latin, and English. However, due to poor health, he dropped out after a year. Two years later, he enrolled at the College of New Jersey, which...if you remember...today’s that’s called Princeton, baby. Oops. Well anyway, after becoming a lawyer later on, he went back to school at John Hopkins University. He earned his PhD there when he was 30. He was a professor for several years before becoming the president of Princeton, his alma mater. His estimated IQ was 145.1, which is the fifth highest of all the Presidents. Warren Harding Harding attended a one-room schoolhouse with his five younger siblings in Caledonia, Ohio. Calvin Coolidge Coolidge was, by most measures, an average student who attended public schools growing up in Vermont. Even though his ambitions were modest, he aimed high for college, attending Amherst College in Massachusetts, graduating with honors there when he was 22. After college, he studied law at a firm in Northampton, Massachusetts, passing the bar exam to become a lawyer around his 27th birthday. His estimated IQ was 127.1, which places him in the lower half of the pack. Herbert Hoover was (“born poor and orphaned young”) and (“hated school, rough childhood”). Regardless, he remained determined to study geology in college, and somehow barely got into the newly established Stanford University in Palo Alto, California. He did well in college, getting involved with lots of extracurricular activities. A few months before they got married, he began law school at Columbia University. He attended two years but never graduated. However, he did pass the bar exam, becoming a lawyer when he was 25. Roosevelt’s estimated IQ was 139.6, which places him in the upper half of the pack. Harry Truman Truman is another President who grew up absolutely loving to read, especially reading about history. The family then moved to New York, where Kennedy attended more private schools. He got mediocre grades. Kennedy went to Choate, a boarding school, for his high school years. He then went on to play football at the University of Michigan, helping them to two undefeated seasons and national titles in 1932 and 1933. He graduated from there with a Bachelor of Arts degree in economics, turning down offers from the Detroit Lions and Green Bay Packers to play professional ball after that. After he graduated high school, he attended the University of Delaware, and continued to be a mediocre student, but he did double major in both history and political science, graduating with a Bachelor of Arts degree before going on to Syracuse University Law School, also as a mediocre student. In conclusion, most of the American Presidents have received at least a college education. Only 12 did not. ALL of them had above average IQs, at least according to the Dr. Simonton study. Oh, at the end of it there will be an opportunity for a Q&A, so that's always fun. So, join me. That seminar and this video were inspired originally by this book here. I've been reading this book since I first started teaching many years ago. It has lots of great scenarios and lots of good tips and it's a great workbook for teachers. I've also put a link to buy it in the description of this video. So, what do you think? How did I do spotting logical fallacies? Was I too hard on Joe and Ben? Let me know in those comments below. Thanks for watching.

### 8. What Was Watergate? (Apr 1, 2022) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HU6biQoPBDk)
**Metrics:** 3.2M views (rounded, list view), 85,266 likes (18:37)
**Opening hook (verbatim, transcript):**
> I'm Mr. Beat Today, whenever the suffix "-gate" is at the end of something, ya know it's bad, man. Travelgate Coingate Climategate Nipplegate Bountygate Gamergate Deflategate Pizzagate Sharpiegate Slapgate Fartgate. Basically, if gate is at the end of a noun, it suggests there might be some widespread scandal or controversy going on.

**Full transcript (verbatim):**
> I’m Mr. Beat Today, whenever the suffix “-gate” is at the end of something, ya know it’s bad, man. Travelgate Coingate Climategate Nipplegate Bountygate Gamergate Deflategate Pizzagate Sharpiegate Slapgate Fartgate Basically, if gate is at the end of a noun, it suggests there might be some widespread scandal or controversy going on. For example, let’s take Fartgate. Yes, this was a real…thing. So the controversy was that someone clearly farted on air when MSNBC’s Chris Matthews was interviewing Congressman Eric Swalwell, but to this day NO ONE has admitted to doing the farting. For the record, I think it was Swalwell who farted. Anyway, how did this whole adding “gate” to the end of a noun to describe a scandal or controversy begin? Well that’s simple. It was the Watergate scandal. Steven: What was the Watergate scandal? Mr. Beat: You really want to know? Steven: Yes! Mr. Beat: You really think you can handle this? Steven: Yes! Mr. Beat: (turning to camera) Fine. Let’s do this. Here’s the story of the Watergate scandal. Once upon a time…well on June 17, 1972, to be exact, five men broke into the Democratic National Committee headquarters in the Watergate Office Building in Washington, D.C. Steven: Oh is that why it’s called “Watergate?” Mr. Beat: Yes that’s why it’s called “Watergate.” Anyway, the five men tried to set up a wiretap and photograph confidential Democratic Party documents. Well, Forrest Gump called up a Watergate security guard named Frank Wills and told him about it. Just kidding. That was just a movie. Forrest Gump is not real. He’s a fake. He’s never existed. He’s made up. Fabricated. But Tom Hanks is real, and thank goodness for that. Anyway, Frank Wills actually noticed tape covering the latches on some of Watergate’s doors, which allowed the doors to close but stay unlocked. He called the police, and they promptly came and arrested the men. Little did most know that these men were connected with the Committee for the Re-Election of the President, or the CRP, or CREEP. Steven: Excuse me, did you call me a creep? Mr. Beat: No I did not. What the heck are you doin’ here? Steven: I don’t belong here The CRP was, officially, a fundraising organization to get Richard Nixon re-elected. Unofficially, they did all kinds of illegal stuff. Ya know, stuff like breaking into Democratic National Committee headquarters. The five men arrested were Virgilio Gonzalez, Bernard Barker, James McCord, Eugenio Martínez, and Frank Sturgis. Four of them were Cuban exiles. They were charged with attempted burglary and attempted interception of communications, basically tryna spy and stuff. Three reporters at the Washington Post: Alfred Lewis, Carl Bernstein, and Bob Woodward, covered the arrests, but later Bernstein and Woodward decided to do a little more digging, and soon discovered that two other dudes were involved with the burglary: Everette Howard Hunt, a former CIA officer, and George Gordon Liddy, a former FBI agent. Huh. And both were creeps! I mean, both worked for CREEP, or the CRP, or the Committee for the Re-Election of the President! And so did James McCord! As a matter of fact, Bernstein and Woodward also discovered that the four Cuban exiles who broke into Watergate had previously worked with the CIA...with Hunt in particular. Well, Bernstein and Woodward had a little help. A mysterious person began providing them important information. Sometimes he called, and sometimes Woodward would meet him in a dark parking garage. The person claimed he was an FBI agent himself and a Washington Post editor later jokingly referred to him simply as “Deep Throat.” Steven: Deep Throat? Mr. Beat: Hey watch your mouth, sonny boy Soon everyone was calling him Deep Throat. As it turns out, the FBI had an investigation into the Watergate break-in, too, and boy was it juicy. For starters, they had found a check made out to the CRP for $25,000 actually deposited in the bank accounts of one of the Watergate burglars, Bernard Baker. Soon the FBI and Bernstein and Woodward had also found out that Hunt and Liddy were destroying all evidence that linked the five men who broke into Watergate to not only the CRP, but to President Richard Nixon himself. Oh, and the higher ups were involved. Liddy’s boss, Jeb Magruder? In on the cover up. Even John freaking Mitchell. I mean John N. Mitchell, who not only led the CRP, but was recently the Attorney General in the Nixon administration, was in on the cover up. There’s no way to know that Nixon himself knew that the CRP did all these shady things, but once he found out that the CRP was connected to Watergate, he worked with his chief of staff, Bob Haldeman, to cover it up. Steven: Wouldn’t that make things worse? Mr. Beat: Abso-lutely Once Nixon and his administration realized the press were looking into them being involved with the Watergate break-in, they immediately began to distance themselves. Nixon’s press secretary, Ron Ziegler, said the President had no comment on a “third-rate burglary attempt.” Nixon ran a public relations campaign to insist he nor his administration, nor even the CRP were connected at all. John Mitchell went as far as having his wife kind of kidnapped so she wouldn’t talk to the press about what she knew about the break-in. I’m serious. Now, Nixon was obviously more concerned about the FBI investigation, and especially more concerned that the FBI was apparently working with Bernstein and Woodward. He met with Haldeman and told him to have the CIA pressure the FBI into dropping its Watergate investigation. Oh this was a recorded conversation, by the way. It was on tape! Crazy enough, the FBI went along with it…for a little while at least. And while Bernstein and Woodward never lost focus, continuing to publish stories describing the CRP’s dirty tricks, most of the media did lose focus, and Nixon easily won re-election in November. The Watergate break-in had hardly any effect on the presidential election of 1972. But despite the landslide victory for Nixon, Watergate wasn’t going to fade away any time soon. Around the same time Nixon was inaugurated, the trial of the Watergate burglars began, with Judge John Sirica presiding. Nixon had a close eye on the trial, meeting with his advisors, the Johns, about concerns that the dudes on trial might speak to the press. John Dean, the White House Counsel, and John Ehrilichman, the Assistant to the President for Domestic Affairs. On January 30, 1973, the Watergate burglars plead guilty or were convicted for conspiracy, burglary, and violation of federal wiretapping laws. Judge Sirica, however, suspected there was a major cover-up, and that the CRP or the President had to know all about the Watergate break-in. His suspicions were confirmed after James McCord wrote a letter to him implicating Nixon and his advisors in the cover-up. Nixon was like “I knew one of them would try this crap!” and called an emergency meeting with John Dean again. Dean urged Nixon to come clean, that the only way to keep everyone else quiet was to pay them off with a million dollars. Nixon replied And this is on tape. This is on tape. Steven: It’s on tape? Mr. Beat: It’s on tape Nixon replied: “You could get a million dollars. And you could get it in cash. I know where it could be gotten.” In other words, Nixon had no intention of ever coming clean on this. Nixon later tried to make it seem he was kidding, but regardless when it was clear Dean wasn’t going along with Nixon, Nixon fired him on April 30, 1973. Well, Dean had been working with the FBI by that time, anyway. Shortly afterward, even though Ehrlichman and Haldeman seemed to still be on board with Nixon, they resigned…well… after Nixon pressured them to, and so did four other Nixon advisors. By now, top officials in the CRP like Jeb Magruder and even John Mitchell were facing indictment, and to attempt to calm down the press, which all of sudden was now paying close attention, Nixon announced that he was not involved with Watergate at all. Well that didn’t ease the minds of the United States Senate. In May, it formed the Watergate Committee, chaired by respected North Carolina senator Sam Ervin. Former Nixon aides told the committee that lots of crimes had happened before the 1972 election intended to undermine the president’s opponents. Well Nixon appointed a Harvard law professor named Archibald Cox to lead a separate, independent investigation. In June, the Watergate Committee called in John Dean. The Tennessee senator Howard Baker famously asked “What did the President know and when did he know it?” Dean spilled the beans. He said he had talked with Nixon about the Watergate coverup many, many times, and that Nixon refused to come clean about it. Nixon was like, nah man, fake news. Dean is making that stuff up. But Americans soon found out that there was a new system in the White House that automatically recorded everything in the Oval Office and Cabinet Room, so if Nixon was in on the Watergate break-in or even if he just knew about it and was trying to cover it up, there could be evidence of it in the recordings. Archibald Cox, as well as the Senate, immediately subpoenaed the White House recordings. However, Nixon refused to release them, citing his “executive privilege” as the president. He even ordered Cox to drop his subpoena, but Cox refused. In response, on Saturday, October 20, 1973, Nixon told his Attorney General Elliot Richardson to fire Cox. Well Richardson refused and immediately resigned. So Nixon then told Richardson’s next in command, Deputy Attorney General William Ruckelshaus to fire him. Well Ruckelshaus also refused and also resigned. So Nixon then told Ruckelshaus’ next in command, Robert Bork, to fire him and Bork was like, “ok fine I’ll fire him.” This whole fiasco became known as the Saturday Night Massacre. Steven: A massacre? That doesn’t make Nixon very good, does it Mr. Beat: No siree, and many Americans began to protest, and after lots of public pressure, Nixon finally agreed to release the tapes. Well, some of them at least. Judge Sirica asked for nine tapes. Nixon gave him seven, and one of those seven tapes…uh…had a big gap of 18 and a half minutes on it that experts say could not have happened accidentally. That was one of the conversations with…you probably guessed it…John Dean. In November, Nixon made a now famous proclamation: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sh163n1lJ4M Still, by early 1974, it seemed pretty clear that President Nixon had at least known about the Watergate break-in and tried to cover it up. There was just little solid evidence to prove it. On March 1, 1974, a grand jury indicted seven former aides of Nixon, who later simply became known as the “Watergate Seven.” That group included the aforementioned John Mitchell, Bob Haldeman, and John Ehrlichman, but additionally Charles Colson, Gordon Strachan, Robert Mardian, and Kenneth Parkinson. In April, the dude who took Cox’s place to investigate the Watergate break-in, Leon Jaworski, had another subpoena ordering Nixon to release 64 additional tapes of his conversations in the Oval Office. Well, instead of releasing the tapes, Nixon just gave them edited transcripts of some of the conversations. Nixon even asked Judge John Sirica to reverse the subpoena, but Sirica denied the motion, saying Nixon had until May 31st to turn over the tapes. Well this turned into a major Supreme Court case called United States v. Nixon, and I actually just released a Supreme Court Briefs video about it so feel free to watch that now. (to Steven) Say, have you watched it yet? Steven: Watched what? Mr. Beat: Nevermind, well long story short, Nixon lost that case Not only had Nixon lost that case, but now it also looked like the House of Representatives was going to impeach him on the charges of obstruction of justice, abuse of power, and contempt of Congress. Woahness. Not only that, Bernstein and Woodward had now even published an entire book that went over their reporting into the whole Watergate Scandal that later became a movie. By August, 57% of Americans wanted Nixon removed from office. Feeling the pressure like never before, and worried about further disgrace, Nixon announced his resignation on August 8, 1974. https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3_DrUaJEBtE&t He became the first and to this date only president in American history to quit the job. The man who replaced him as President, Gerald Ford, later gave him a full pardon, so Nixon never faced any criminal punishment. For the rest of his life, he maintained his innocence. But others in his administration? Yeah that’s a different story. In total, 69 people were indicted and 48 people convicted for either participating in, aiding in, or covering up information about the Watergate break-in. Again, MANY of these folks were top Nixon administration officials. Some top dogs even ended up serving prison time. Most of the 48 convicted served prison time. Congress moved to further reduce the power of the Presidency, passing laws like the Privacy Act and creating committees to oversee the shenanigans of executive branch agencies. In 2005, Americans learned that “Deep Throat,” ya know, the dude who provided Bernstein and Woodward all the important information for their reporting, was an FBI agent named Mark Felt. Steven: So his real name wasn’t Deep Throat? Mr. Beat: I’m afraid not. The Watergate Scandal is arguably the biggest political scandal in American history. It led to a major setback for the Republican Party, especially as they lost many seats in Congress in the 1974 midterm elections. Watergate dramatically increased cynicism and distrust of the federal government, which had already been on the rise due to its lies revolving around the Vietnam War. But it also proved that the American system of checks and balances actually worked. The judicial and legislative branches were able to fight corruption within the executive branch, and ever since the Presidents have been a bit more cautious, I’d argue. Well, most of them have been a bit more cautious. We’ll leave it at that. So if Watergate happened today, how would Americans react to it? I tend to think it’d be quite a bit different as we were a lot less partisan in the 1970s, but let me know in those comments.Hey if you’re watching this video right now and you’re looking for a free tutor or if you want to tutor yourself, check out Connect Me, a non profit organization meant to help students grades K-8. They’re doing great work. I’ve put a link in the description for you to check them out. Thanks to my brother Steven for helping me make this video, and thank YOU for watching.

**Structure:** Straight historical explainer
**Framing:** Present-day linguistic-meme hook (the "-gate" suffix) used to bridge into the origin-story explainer

### 9. How Much Wealth Every President Had (Sep 4, 2020) [link](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=G-fYO5e7FN8)

> I'm Mr. Beat. We Americans, we only elect people to public office who represent us.
